| 2026-11-01 | **Zombies: everyone one team against waves of zombies round the house, together and alone** (Yoshi's request: "multiplayers can join one team and beat the zombies in waves") | **How it plays.** The admin starts Zombies from the admin tab, like the other party games. Everyone is gathered at the house's outside places, and the edge round the two houses holds. Nobody travels, drives, rides or flies. The house and the bungalow are open together and shut alone, as at any time (the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below). A car would keep its driver out of reach for good, and nobody should run zombies over at a family game, so the zombies walk round the parked cars instead. The first wave comes after 8 seconds. After each wave there is a break of 15 seconds, with a countdown in the game's bar. A wave brings 3 + 2n zombies, times 1 for one player, 2.2 for four, 3.8 for eight and 5 for sixteen. At most 11 are up at once alone, 20 for four and 30 from eight players. They start at 0.65 m/s, half of walking, and gain 0.06 a wave up to 2.3, never as fast as running (3.5). That keeps their walk clip near its own pace; from 0.95 m/s they lurch. They have 50 health, 12 more each wave: two pistol shots at first. Every fifth wave brings a big one (two in the tenth, at most three): slow, eight times as tough, and it pounds the ground round it. A zombie goes for the nearest player who fights, round walls, fences, hedges and the cars, and into the houses after them (the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below). Next to you it swipes after a wind-up you can step away from: 10 health, one more each wave, at most 25. A player just hit can't be hit again for 0.4 s, so a crowd doesn't melt anyone at once. Whoever goes down waits for the end of the wave and comes back at the house. Reconnecting doesn't bring them back early. When everyone who fights is down at once, the game is over. The end screen shows the wave reached, each player's zombies beaten and the best wave ever, kept in the database like the rules. Explorers aren't chased. They get a card with one-tap Paintball and Action buttons, and a line in the chat. The strongmen and clowns stand aside until the game ends. Alone, the Play tab has a Zombies button: the same game runs in the page, and the best wave is kept in the browser. **What you see.** The bar shows the wave, the zombies left and who still stands, or the countdown. A banner shows as each wave begins. Zombies are small green dots on the minimap. Hit, a zombie staggers in a puff of grey-green dust; beaten, it falls over and fades. In Paintball (chosen, or the admin's child-friendly rule) the paint sticks where it hit and moves with the body, and a beaten zombie vanishes in a puff of paint. The words are the friendly ones ("tagged", "splattered", "Iedereen is geraakt"). No blood, ever. They groan now and then, quietly, from where they stand, and swish when they swipe. The kill feed puts it in plain words: "Robin beat a zombie · Rifle", "A zombie caught you". Other players' regular zombies stay out of it (sixteen players beat over a hundred in a wave); their big ones show. During the game the scoreboard (Tab) shows each player's zombies beaten and times down. The meetup scoreboard ranks everyone by zombies beaten. **The models** are our own, built headless by `tools/zombies.py` in Blender: a thin one in a torn shirt, a lanky teen in a hoodie, a stocky mechanic in an overall, a farmer in dungarees, wellies and a flat cap, and the big one. Comic faces with big mismatched eyes and one tooth, muted green and grey skin, vertex colours and one material. Each has a 20-bone armature with the same names in all, weights worked out in the script, and a far version built again from fewer segments. The clips are made in the script too and kept in place: walk, lurch, idle, swipe, flinch and fall, and the big one's stomp and pound. A regular one has 3,800 to 4,000 triangles, the big one 5,400, a far one about 1,200. The file is 1.1 MB (0.4 MB sent with brotli), loaded with the game. **How it's built.** `shared/zombies.mjs` holds the waves and the zombies, run by the server together and by the page alone. They walk on a grid of 0.5 m squares over the party area. A square is closed by a wall, hedge, fence or house standing 0.4 to 2.2 m above the ground, by a parked car, or by the edge; round the houses a finer grid with every floor takes over (the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below). Every side of a block is drawn in, so a fence 5 cm thick closes its squares. The grid takes about 40 ms, once a game. Twice a second a distance field from the players (Dial's algorithm over the grid) gives each zombie its next square; it stops once every zombie's square is settled. Close by, in a clear line, a zombie walks straight at you. They keep apart. They come out of sight, never closer than 13 m, only where the grid joins up with the players: from all over the party area since the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below (first along its edge, about 26 m away). The server sends them with the strongmen's ticks, ten a second: id, place, heading, what it does with its kind, its health and (since the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below) its floor, about 35 bytes each. **Drawing them.** When the file loads, every clip of every kind is sampled 30 times a second into one half-float picture of bone matrices (60 texels by 1,235 rows, 0.6 MB on the graphics card; the values are a few metres at most). Each kind and its far version (beyond 24 m) is one instanced mesh. Its vertex shader skins every zombie at its own frame, blending two frames, or two clips for a fifth of a second. Walks play at the pace their feet keep, measured from the clip. They stay out of the ambient occlusion pass, whose plain material can't pose them. A soft patch on the ground stands in for their real-time shadow, as under the players' figures away from the house. Their hit boxes are like a player's: the body to the shoulders, and the head on its head bone. For paint, the hit's spot on the posed body is found on the processor and turned back to the rest pose; the fragment shader paints up to eight spots a zombie. **Security.** The server keeps every zombie, its health and every swipe. Hit forgery (a page claiming hits it didn't make): a shot's message (zhit) is checked as a hit on a strongman is. The weapon must be carried and held, within its fire rate (70% of its delay, one clock), by a player alive, armed, and not in a car or on the school grounds. The zombie must be up and within 100 m on the server's positions; no zombie twice, and no more pellets than the gun fires. The damage is the weapon's own. A forged or stale id does nothing and tells nobody. Replaying a hit on a fallen zombie scores nothing. Flooding: the fire-rate clock on top of the flood guard (40 messages a second). Blasts are set off on the server (a rocket) or checked there (a grenade), as before. Position spoofing to escape: no flying, no cars or horses, the server holds the edge, and out of every zombie's reach for long is out of the wave (the row "Zombies: the houses open together" below; before it, the houses' ground floors were held shut). Respawn abuse: a downed player who reconnects is still down. Player against player is refused: one team. Parameter tampering: ids and pellet counts must be whole numbers, not negative, at most 8 zombies a shot, and the weapon is looked up with `Object.hasOwn`. The best wave read back from the database is checked (a whole number, at most 16 short names). **Cost.** Drawing: 30 zombies in view in the street (one big one), A/B in one page, the same view with them shown and hidden, alternating, each render timed on the graphics chip (medians of 30 pairs). On Low (704 × 440) they add 0.94 ms; on Medium (1024 × 640) 1.01 ms. With real-time shadows it was 2.53 ms on Low, almost all of it the shadow pass, hence the patches. The processor's work for 30 animated zombies is 0.04 ms a frame. Their shaders compile in the background (`precompile`) once the models and the sky's light are in, and again as a game begins; the bone picture goes up to the graphics card then too. Measured in a fresh browser profile: without it the frame the first zombies appear in took 67 ms (one shader compiled on the spot). With it the worst frame round the first wave is 19 ms, as in 5 quiet seconds before, and no shader is compiled. At most 11 draws: five kinds near and far, and the patches. Server: 16 bots round the house shooting through zhit, on the server's own process. A tick takes 0.9 to 1.2 ms in all; sending to 16 pages is 0.6 to 0.8 ms of it, as in free play, and the zombies 0.13 to 0.24 ms (the distance field 0.10 to 0.19). The process uses 2.2 to 2.5% of a core. Each page gets 21.5 KB/s with 30 zombies up (8.1 KB/s of it zombies), against 14.9 in free play. |
| 2026-11-01 | **The end house of the new terrace at Molenhoek at the street's level: the row's floor raised by 52 cm** (Yoshi: "this house is not at the right street level height") | **Cause.** The street model takes a house's floor from the village block it replaces, 5 cm above the block's foot. The village sets each block's foot 30 cm below the lowest ground round its walls, so that a block never floats on a slope. The row's floor therefore lay 25 cm below the lowest ground under the end house and 36 cm below the paving at its front door. The pavement ran over the foot of the door and the wall, and the tiled floor inside lay below it. The other four houses of the row were as low (34 to 53 cm under the ground at their doors): the row shares one floor level. It has been so since the house was built; the brick house at the lane's corner left the row's data as it was. **Raised, not the ground lowered.** A new house is built to its street: its floor goes a little above the pavement, so that no water runs in. Lowering the paving would leave a step against the street's kerb and the neighbours, and cut into the street's surfaces and the portal's spot. **The level.** The row keeps one floor level, as built, with its bands and window heads straight along it. It stands 15 cm above the relief in front of its five front doors on average (`threshold` in tools/molenhoek_survey.py `plan`). The end house's threshold is now 16 to 17 cm above the paving at its door, the middle three 16 to 18 cm. The relief's 4 m grid rises 20 cm towards the row's first house, whose door sits within 3 cm of the ground in front. **At the doors.** A dark stone step at each front door reaches down into the pavement (`down`). A ramp of grey pavers leads up to the end house's garage door (`ramp`). Both are walked on. The walk-in drops its front door that was 25 cm higher for walking through than it shows. **Inside and the garden.** Everything inside is measured from the floor and moves with it: the stair, doors, glass, furniture and blocks. The garden stays at the floor's level, flush with the glass doors: the terrace 2 cm under it, the lawn 4 cm. Behind the house the relief falls away, so the garden now lies 9 to 42 cm above it. The fences' gravel boards and the wire fence's base plates reach from under the lower ground to over the higher one, so no gap shows from either side. The ground is still cut out under the house and the garden. The shed rose with the lawn, and its roof stood 80 cm over the street fence. It looked right only because the lawn lay too low. Its walls are now 1.90 m high at the street and 1.85 m at the garden, its door 1.73 m. Its roof shows 30 to 40 cm over the fence, as before. **Checked.** Alone the front door stays locked. Together you open it with E and walk in from the pavement, up the step without a jump. You walk out through the sliding door onto the terrace. Leaving the others from the garden puts you at the End of Molenhoek portal. The portal still lands you on the pavement. The `/location` spots and the game's edge build the same. The other four street models build byte for byte as before, and so does the third one without the row's five houses. The street models' other houses take their floor the same way; they are left as they were. |
| 2026-11-01 | **The sky's black flicker on Apple: no surface normal of zero length, and no name card in the ambient occlusion** | Yoshi saw the sky "sometimes flicker black" on a Mac and an iPhone, never on this PC. There were two causes. Nothing went past the half floats' end (65504): the brightest pixel anywhere was the sun, at 59 936. **1. NaN under the sky, on every preset.** The lighting normalises each surface's normal for every pixel, and a normal of no length gives NaN (not a number). The far pieces of the ground hang a 2 m skirt round their edge, so no gap shows where they meet a nearer piece. The skirt is drawn from both sides on the same corners. Its two sides cancelled, so its lower corners had a normal of zero length. Far off, the skirt is a sliver under a pixel high. The pixels that land on it came out NaN in the scene's half-float picture. That made a row along the far edge of the ground, just under the sky, which changes as you move or turn. The open data's outlines also held triangles with no area (paving, gravel, roads, gables), with zero normals on their corners. This PC (ANGLE on Direct3D) gets the same NaN pixels, 130 to 180 in a view over the fields. Its tone mapping turned them light grey, lost in the haze, and the output pass's guard from this morning turns them white. Chromium's SwiftShader treats NaN and infinity as Apple's chips do. With the guard taken out (Metal may compile with fast maths, which can drop the guard's NaN test), it draws them black, and the edge smoothing spreads them. **Fix:** the skirt's lower corners take the normal of the ground above them (`world/terrain.ts`). `world/normals.ts` gives any other corner left at zero the normal of its triangle's other corners, or straight up. The ground, the blocks, the detailed roads and the neighbours' roofs use it. **2. A dark wedge in the sky by the portals' names, on High and Ultra.** An Apple chip's name makes the graphics test start on High or Ultra. This PC's test doesn't pick High: a frame there takes over 20 ms, and the test wants 14. The ambient occlusion first draws the scene with one plain material, for depth and normals. A sprite (a portal's name, a player's name tag) turns to face the eye in its own shader, which that material lacks. So it was drawn as a flat card standing in the world, and the sky behind the card was darkened. The wedge swept round as you turned: by the church's portal, 900 to 1,500 more bright pixels darkened by over 40%, at every heading within 35 degrees of the name. **Fix:** sprites stay out of that pass, as its own points and lines do (`main.ts`). **Checked.** No mesh has a triangle on a zero normal any more, at home or in the village. In SwiftShader every pass of 142 views is free of NaN and infinity: Medium, High and Ultra, in the fields by the house and on the church square, looking all round, up and into the sun, with the chimney smoke in view. Before the fix, 33 of the 84 views in the fields had NaN pixels. On the church square there were none, as the houses hide the far ground. On a slow turn with the guard taken out, 77 of 184 frames had NaN before the fix and none after. On Direct3D the picture is unchanged apart from these two: where neither showed, the mean difference is 0.001 to 0.06 of 255 on Medium and within the noise between two runs on High. **Cost.** None on the graphics card. On Direct3D on High, with the two versions measured in turn 12 times at three views, the fixed one took 0.91 to 1.00 times as long (medians). The normals are checked once as the world is built: 10 to 20 ms for all 2.7 million corners, spread over the tiles. |
| 2026-11-01 | **Zombies: the houses open together, the zombies follow you in and upstairs; runners; zombies from all round** (Yoshi: "this should be open in multiplayer zombie mode", "make some zombies run really fast", "make them spawn at more random places") | **How it plays.** Together the house and the bungalow are open during Zombies, as at any time together: doors and sliding doors work and panes can be shot out. Alone they stay shut as before. A house is no place to hide. The zombies follow you in, up the stairs to the second floor, up the outside stair onto the roof terrace (alone too), into the bungalow and up its stairs. A shut door doesn't stop them: a zombie stands at it for 0.6 s and shoves it open, and every page sees it swing, as when a player opens it. Doorways are always open to them, so a player holding a door shut never makes a safe room: the zombie walks through, and the next one shoves it again. A pane shot out lets them through; mended, it closes again. A swipe never goes through a wall, a floor, a whole pane or a shut door. The server follows the line from the zombie's chest to the player's every 10 cm through the house's own blocks (outdoors, the tall things on its grid). A player more than 2.1 m above a zombie's feet or 0.6 m below is out of its reach, and a blast hurts zombies on its own floor only. **Out of reach.** A flood fill with the page's own movement rules (a circle of 0.25 m, 1.8 m tall, steps of 0.5 m, jumps of 0.5 m more) found 172,865 spots a player can reach in the party area, every 25 cm. The zombies can get at every one of them in and round both houses: 47,416 together with the doors open, and 29,678 alone. That includes the tops of fittings and furniture: a zombie swipes from the floor beside them. One spot is left: a gap between two neighbours' buildings by the bungalow, wide enough for a player and too narrow for the zombies' half-metre squares outdoors. The rule for it, for anything like it, and for a changed page that claims to hover or to stand in a wall: a player no zombie can get at for 2 s during a wave is told "The zombies can't reach you there. Go where they can within 10 seconds, or you're out of this wave." Ten seconds more and they are out of the wave, as if caught, and everyone is told. Out rather than pulled back: being moved away from a crowd would be a free escape, and this way the same rule answers position spoofing. **Runners.** From wave 3 some zombies run: 12% of the wave, 2% more each wave, at most 30%. They are always the teen in the hoodie, who no longer walks, so a hoodie means a runner. They run 3.0 to 3.4 m/s against a player's 3.5: you just outrun one in a straight line, and they turn wide. They have half a walker's health (two pistol shots at wave 3). Their swipe has the same 0.45 s wind-up as everyone's, so one that reaches you still warns you first. Their sprint is a clip of its own, made in `tools/zombies.py` like the others: a third of the loop on each foot, so both are off the ground between strides; knees high, leaning in, arms pumping. It covers 3.2 m/s and the planted feet slide 0.0 mm. The first runners of a game get a heads-up: "Runners!" ("Ze rennen!") in the banner and the chat. **Where they come from.** Spots every 2 m over the open ground of the party area, with no wall, hedge, car or fence within a square: 2,076, worked out with the grid at the start. None in the houses: a zombie appearing in a room would be unfair, and the doors let them in anyway. Each zombie picks one at random, weighted: most 15 to 28 m from the nearest player, fewer up to 40 m, never closer than 13 m, never in anyone's sight (glass is seen through now), and only where the grid joins it to a player. A side round the players with fewer of this wave's zombies weighs more, so they come from several sides at once, and the last few spots hardly at all. **How it's built.** Round each house, 1 m wider, the grid has squares of 0.25 m with every floor in them: a floor wherever a block's top (or the ground) has 1.75 m of room over it, and two floors side by side join when they are at most 0.5 m apart (a stair's steps, the bungalow's 45 cm threshold). The outdoor grid hands over to it at its edge. Together the doors' moving leaves are left out (their fixed halves stay in); alone every leaf is a wall. The server sends each zombie's floor in its tick, and the page puts it on the step under it there. **Security.** The server never refused door toggles during Zombies; the pages locked the doors. Now they work as at any time together: at most 5 a second per player (each one goes to every page), and only an id of the right form. A zombie's shove is the server's own, at most once in 1.5 s per door. Panes keep the limits they always had: at most 200 broken, numbers under 4000, and the flood guard. A forged pane number opens nothing (only the house's own panes are in the grid), and the grid's regions are worked out again at most once per field. The check that kept players out of the houses during the game goes, since they are open. Position spoofing (a changed page claiming a height or a spot in a wall out of reach) meets the out-of-reach rule. Hit forgery, the fire rate, flooding and parameter tampering are checked as before. **Cost.** The grid: 41 ms at the start of a game (cold, median of 11) against 39 ms before; alone in the page 23 ms. The earlier load (16 bots shooting at the pistol's pace) replayed in the server's own process on a simulated clock, the code before and after measured the same way, per tick and averaged over a minute: the distance field 33 to 37 µs against 22 to 23, the whole horde 53 to 55 µs against 34 to 37, the tick 141 to 148 µs against 128 to 131. The field skips the houses' grids unless a player or a zombie is in one. It costs more mostly because zombies now come from all sides, so the furthest along the way is further. With 8 of 16 players inside and zombies in the houses, a field takes 0.56 ms (median). Each page gets 0.2 to 1.3 KB/s more (the floors). On the page the runners use the hoodie's own mesh: no extra draw (7 for 11 zombies in view), the bone picture 20 rows taller (1,255), the model file 7.8 KB bigger. |

The reach is measured to that spot, and they get up there. **Penalty evasion:** a player put out of the wave for standing out of every zombie's reach (a perch, or a page claiming to hover) can't be helped up, which would undo it. | 2026-11-02 | **The game night page: brieltje.be/inschrijven** (Yoshi: "inschrijven voor de game night formulier", "the 'inschrijven' pagina will be the actual sign up for when the event is about to be organized") | Two pages, two jobs. /interested only asks whether people would come. /inschrijven is the registration for the night itself, once it is being organised. It asks for a name, a first name, an email address, how many come to play and how many to watch (players count for the laptops and PCs), and ideas or wishes (optional), with the same 13-or-older tick as the other forms. The place, the date and the time are set by the admin on the admin page's new Game night tab. Until then the page shows "Nog te bepalen" and says what isn't set yet will be emailed. A registration gives a private link to look at it or cancel it, as on the interest page. The tab shows the totals (registrations, players, spectators, people), searches, copies the addresses for Bcc, deletes one or all. The privacy policy has a section for it: legitimate interest, kept until cancelled and at most a month after the night (the "Delete all" button). **Security.** *CSRF*: every change (signing up, cancelling, the admin's details and deletes) only accepts a same-origin JSON body (Origin must match Host, Content-Type JSON, at most 4 KB), so a form on another site can't post one. *Stored XSS*: what is typed is kept as plain text and both pages draw it with textContent only; a name like `<img onerror=…>` shows as text (checked in the browser). Control characters and text direction overrides (U+202E and the like) are taken out, so a name or an address can't read backwards on the admin page. *Guessing someone's registration (IDOR)*: the own link carries a 144-bit secret after the #, sent in the X-Gamenight-Token header (never in a URL a log keeps), stored only as a SHA-256 hash and compared in constant time; a wrong secret or another id gets 404. *Spam and floods*: a trap field, 5 registrations an hour per address and 300 a day in all, counts limited to whole numbers from 0 to 10. *BREACH*: the API answers stay uncompressed (no-transform), as all API answers. Admin calls need the admin's session. 49 API checks and 17 browser checks passed on a local server. |

A row is sharpened poles with pale cut tips, lashed to a rail, each standing on the ground under it; three knocks as it goes in. The Controls tab lists it, and the claymore now too. **Security.** The server decides. *Forged pickups*: as for the other rewards (the bundle must be there and within 1.3 m of the server's position; it is taken once; a full pocket leaves it for someone else). *Placement*: stakes carried; within 2.4 m of the server's position and 0.8 m above or below it; the middle on a floor the zombies walk. Values that aren't finite numbers are refused. On a local server: a try with none carried is answered with its reason, forged messages plant nothing, and 60 sent at once get one answer. *Wear and slowing*: on the server, every tick, against its own zombie positions; a page can't wear or mend a row. **Cost.** Server: 32 rows and 30 zombies take 6.7 µs a tick. A row is nine numbers in the game's view, sent when one is planted, a stake falls or a row goes. Page: all stakes are one instanced mesh and all rails another, so two draws (and two in the shadow pass) however many rows. 32 rows in view (146 stakes), shown and hidden in turns in one page, medians of 16 pairs: +0.04 ms on Medium (1024 × 576) and +0.05 ms on Low (704 × 396), within the noise. |

Shots now pass through it (checked: no part of the gun in hand catches a shot, and an aimed shot lands on the house behind). **Where it sits.** It was fitted to the gun's bounding box, muzzle flash included, which reaches far past the barrel: it sat out over the muzzle, fat, with a glowing lens, like a torch. Dont overpower them, just as a cool temp helper, dropped by the big zombies") | **How it plays.** A big zombie leaves a turret beside its stakes and its reward, the three a little apart. Carry one. Hold Use (E, △, the touch Use button) for half a second where there is nothing else to use (no door in reach, nobody to help up), and it goes up a metre in front of you. One up per player, eight in all. It unfolds for 1.2 s, then turns (3.5 rad/s) to the nearest zombie it can see within 16 m, on its own floor or near it, with nothing in between from its head to the zombie's chest (the zombies' grid, as for a blast), and shoots when it faces it: 12 health every 0.3 s. It keeps to its zombie while it sees it, unless another comes within 60% of the distance. After 100 rounds it is spent and folds away in a puff. It never hurts a player, and zombies don't go for it. **Not overpowered.** 40 health a second against the rifle's 260 and the pistol's 212: about a sixth of a rifle. Its 100 rounds are 1,200 health in all, about ten zombies at wave 5, fewer later (its damage doesn't grow with the waves). Its hits don't make a zombie flinch: a regular bullet staggers one for 0.35 s, and at a shot every 0.3 s that would have held its zombie still for good. In two tests at wave 5, with nobody else shooting, its 100 rounds beat 6 and 10 zombies. A zombie it beats counts for its owner, and the kill feed says "Turret". **What you see and hear.** A purple ring for the drop, a small turret turning above it. Up: a tripod with an olive head (pink in Paintball), an ammo can at its side and a barrel; the head rises out of the hub as it unfolds, turns to each zombie it shoots and flashes; between shots it looks slowly round. Its eye shows the rounds left: green, amber past half, blinking red in the last fifth. Each shot is a soft rifle crack from where it stands (only within 45 m) and a puff on the zombie, paint in Paintball. The HUD shows "Turret: hold E" while you carry one. **Security.** The server decides everything. *Placement*: carried, within 2.4 m of the server's position and 0.8 m above or below it, on a floor the zombies walk, a metre from another turret; one try every 400 ms on top of the flood guard; never while down, unarmed, in a car or where shooting is off; values that aren't finite numbers are refused. *Aim and hits*: on the server, every tick, against its own zombie positions; no page says what a turret hit, so there is nothing to forge. *Pickups*: as the other rewards (there, within reach of the server's position, taken once; a second turret stays for someone else). On a local server: a try with none carried is answered with its reason, and forged messages set nothing up. **Cost.** Server: eight turrets and 30 zombies take 4.6 to 7.9 µs a tick (a look along the grid only when it may shoot, at the nearest four at most). Each tick's shots go to every page in one message, [turret, zombie, rounds left], at most ten a second. Page: four draws a turret, five while it flashes, so at most 40 with eight up; their frame time wasn't measured on its own (the claymores' 44 draws had no measurable cost). |

| 2026-11-02 | **The furniture planner goes; spawning with /name instead, off at first together** (Yoshi: "De 'meubel' spawn functie mag weg. Ik wil wel dat iedereen dingen kan spawnen met /[object], tenzij de admin het uitschakelt (staat by default off) voor iedereen in multiplayer"; asked, Yoshi chose: the planner entirely, picking up and turning included, and off by default together) | **How it works.** In the chat, a thing's name makes it appear in front of you: a piece of furniture 2 m ahead, its front to you, square to where you look; a clown 3 m ahead and a strongman 5 m; a Tesla side-on where it fits (4.5, 6 or 3.5 m ahead, as the server checks a parking spot). A name may be English or Dutch, or the item's id: every word typed must start a word of the name (/sofa, /stoel, /tesla red, /tesla rood, /bed 160, /krachtpatser, /reus; a few aliases such as /zetel, /bank, /tv). Of those that fit, an id or a whole word counts first, then the plainest (shortest) name. A bare /name spawns only when every word is a whole word of a name, so /church, /kerk, /school and /huis still travel; /spawn sofa takes the longer way, /spawn alone lists every thing by kind. /remove (or /weg, /verwijder, /delete) takes away the nearest thing you look at within 12 m: furniture, a spawned Tesla, or a character. Each spawn says so in the chat with how to take it away. Alone everything is allowed and kept in the browser, as before. Together the admin can always; everyone else only when the admin ticks "Players can spawn things with /name" in the settings, which is off at first (it replaces "Players can use the furniture planner", which was on). The chat is a keyboard's, so on touch and the controller there is no spawning; the Controls tab lists /name and /remove for the keyboard. **What went.** The planner (P, its button in the Play tab, its bar, its touch buttons Place, Move, Next, Turn, Remove and Done, its keys in the Controls tab) and with it picking up, moving and turning pieces. "Reset furniture" (alone, and the admin's) and the admin's "Remove all furniture" stay. **Security.** The server decides. *Authorisation*: placing furniture, a Tesla or a character needs the rule or the admin; it is checked per message, so turning the rule off stops it at once. *Position*: a spawn must be within 8 m of where the server has the player (a Tesla 11 m: its middle is further out) and 3 m above or below, inside the edge that holds now; before, the planner's pieces had no distance check at all, so a changed page could put furniture anywhere on the map. *Ownership*: by the session that spawned a thing, kept on the server only (never sent: a session key is a secret), not by the name it shows: a player takes away only what their own session spawned, the admin anything. A first version went by the name; an automated security review found two ways round it: a guest calling themselves "layout" could take away the starting layout (stored as by "layout"), and once a guest's session ended (their name is theirs only while it lasts, "taken today") a newcomer with the same name would inherit their things and their count. Both are closed and checked on a local server; after a restart only the admin takes away what was spawned before. Moving a piece by its id is no longer accepted from anyone but the admin's Teslas. *Flooding and filling up*: furniture messages at most one every 400 ms (each is a database write), characters likewise, Teslas on their own clock as before; at most 20 pieces, 2 Teslas and 2 characters per player, on top of the totals (300 pieces, 12 Teslas, the clowns' maximum). *Tampering*: the item and kind must be in the catalogue (an own-property lookup), every number finite. **Checks.** Alone in the browser: the names above find the right things and places stay places; /sofa, /tesla rood and /clown appear in front of you and are kept in the browser; /weg removes the sofa you look at; /remove at the sky says to look at something; /spawn lists furniture, cars and characters; an unknown name says so; P does nothing and there is no planner left. On a local server with the admin and a guest who joined with the PIN: off at first, the guest is refused furniture, clowns and Teslas; on, the guest's sofa appears in front of them, theirs; 30 m away is too far; a move is ignored; the admin's chair isn't the guest's to take away, their own sofa is; the third character and the 21st piece are refused; forged values spawn nothing; off again, refused; a guest's Tesla is refused where it doesn't fit (with why) and placed where it does, theirs to take away. |

| 2026-11-03 | **The sky box sits exactly on the far plane, not a hair in front of it** (a Mac user's debug report: "a large part of the sky is pure black, bounded by a sharp straight edge... appears and disappears as the camera turns") | The 1 November fix (row above, 2026-11-01) took the NaN row along the far ground and the dark wedge by the portal names, but the black flicker in the sky stayed on Apple machines. A Mac user reproduced it in Chrome on an M5 Pro (ANGLE on Metal, the same layer Safari and iPhones use) and measured it: the sky's vertex shader put the box at `gl_Position.z = gl_Position.w * 0.9999999`, and for about one heading in three (23 of 68 at the church on Ultra, 66 of 244 on Low, 38 of 192 at home) Metal dropped whole triangles of the box, so nothing was drawn there and the cleared black showed through, switching on and off with a turn of one degree. The scene target held exactly 0 in the hole: no NaN, no infinity. Changing only that constant to 1.0 (also 0.99999 or 0.9999) gave 0 bad headings everywhere; a constant sky colour, no post-processing, no half floats, frozen clouds, no adaptive resolution and the sky drawn first all changed nothing. A likely mechanism, not proven in the driver: 0.9999999 rounds to two float steps under 1, and Metal's clip range maps a corner near or behind the eye to a point outside both the near and the far plane, so the triangle goes. **Now** `z = w`: depth exactly 1.0, the value the buffer is cleared to, so with the default less-or-equal test the sky still fills exactly the pixels nothing else covered. It is what three.js uses for its own background box and cube. 0.99999 would have put the sky in front of anything beyond about 1.7 km (0.9999: 670 m, inside the 725 m draw distance), so the exact value is the right one. **Checked** on this PC: Direct3D on Ultra and SwiftShader on Low, six headings round the church's bad one, 0 black sky pixels before and after; the picture is unchanged here. Not checked: a real Mac, Safari, iPhone or iPad. The reporter checked the production build in Chrome on the Mac: black without the change, whole with it, black again with it taken out. SwiftShader never showed this one, so for Apple's clipping a Mac in Chrome is the test. |

Releasing the trigger discards the remainder. Extra spread stays with each weapon, decays while it is holstered and clears when magazines are refilled after respawning. Damage, recoil and the weapon-switch delay stay as chosen. **Security:** no messages or server checks change; the rifle's shortest corrected interval is 70 percent of its configured delay, matching the existing server rate guard. |

| 2026-11-05 | **GPU-compressed textures (KTX2, Basis Universal), phones at full size** (Yoshi: "compressed textures (KTX2)... phones get full-size textures instead of half-size ones") | The 76 surface textures decoded to plain pixels take about 416 MB of graphics memory with their mip chains, so phones got half-size WebP files. Every texture now also exists as a KTX2 file (`web/public/textures/ktx2/`, made by `tools/ktx2.py` from the full-size WebP files with the encoder basisu v2.10 run by Node as WASI, fetched into `tools/.bin/`). It stays compressed on the chip: BC7 on desktops (about 104 MB), ETC2 and ASTC on phones and Apple (about 69 MB, less than the 104 MB the half-size WebP set took). Colour and roughness maps are ETC1S; normal maps are UASTC with RDO and Zstandard, because ETC1S flattened the brick joints and roof tiles at a grazing light; the six normal maps of plain noise (grass, soil, road, asphalt, gravel, hedge) are ETC1S, which looked the same in the game at a quarter of the size. The files are stored flipped, so screenshots match the WebP path. `world/textureFiles.ts` loads them for all three texture loaders, and falls back to the WebP files (half size on phones) without WebAssembly, without a compressed format, when the transcoder doesn't start within 10 s (checked with a 313-byte texture first) or when one file fails; `?webp` forces the WebP path. Cost: the download grows from 10.3 MB (half set 2.5 MB) to 19.0 MB for everyone. On an emulated 10 Mbit/s phone the start took 19.4 s instead of 7.8 s; at 50 Mbit/s on a desktop it took 0.6 to 1.1 s longer. Frame times did not change measurably on this PC. **Security (CSP relaxation, script injection):** script-src gains `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` only, which permits compiling WebAssembly and nothing else: `eval()`, `new Function()` and inline scripts stay blocked. The module is our own same-origin file (connect-src `'self'`). three's transcoder script built its bindings through `new Function()`, which needs `'unsafe-eval'`; those two functions in `web/public/basis/basis_transcoder.js` were rewritten as plain closures (noted in the file, NOTICE.txt and the credits, as Apache 2.0 asks) so the policy stays without it. The `.wasm` gets a brotli copy at build time (527 to 204 KB): a static file without secrets or visitor input, so BREACH doesn't apply. |

| 2026-11-06 | **Private data lives in the public repository as sealed files that only the production server opens** (Yoshi: "i want you to detect the homes, it's bungalow, [the end house at Molenhoek] and the main house in briel, I want the protections to apply to those 3 with minimal manual work from my end!"; asked whether to make the repository private, chose "Keep it public"; asked where the key comes from: "Derive it from ADMIN_PASSWORD") | The insides of the three locked homes must leave the public files without a manual step for Yoshi. They are committed as `web/private/<name>.enc` (ECIES: an X25519 key per file, HKDF-SHA256, AES-256-GCM, the file name as authenticated data; format in `web/server/privateCrypto.mjs`). The server derives its key pair from `ADMIN_PASSWORD` with scrypt (N=2^15, r=8, p=1) at start and opens the files into memory (`web/server/privateData.mjs`); a plain copy in the git-ignored `web/.private/` wins on a development PC. The public key is in `web/shared/private-key.json` and at `GET /api/private/key`, so an agent seals with `node tools/private_data.mjs seal` and needs no secret. After Yoshi changes `ADMIN_PASSWORD` the admin's Homes tab says the insides can't be opened, and an agent runs `node tools/private_data.mjs reseal`. The manifest holds the SHA-256 of each plain file so a re-run skips unchanged files and diffs stay small. **Security**: offline attack on the committed files: the private key never leaves the server and is never stored, but it is only as strong as `ADMIN_PASSWORD`, so that password must be long and random (a guessable one lets anyone with the repository open the files offline). Old commits keep the files sealed to the old key, so a leaked old password opens old files; rotating the password protects only files sealed after the reseal. The public key endpoint is harmless (it only lets anyone seal), is cacheable for 5 minutes and sends no personal data. Tampering, a swapped file name and a wrong key all fail GCM authentication, and the server skips such a file and logs its name once. `GET /api/admin/private` is admin only and returns names and the two key ids, never contents. Without `ADMIN_PASSWORD` there is no key pair, nothing opens and the key endpoint answers 404. |
| 2026-11-06 | **The private data has a key of its own, not the admin password** (Yoshi: chose "Separate random key, set by me") | The first version derived the key pair from `ADMIN_PASSWORD` with scrypt. A security review found, right after it went live, that the public key (and later every sealed file) then let anyone test admin-password guesses offline, past the login's limits. The public key endpoint was taken down meanwhile. Now the Railway variable `PRIVATE_DATA_KEY` holds 32 random bytes (base64, 44 characters, or 64 hex) and is the raw X25519 private key, with no key derivation. The parser is strict: anything else (a passphrase, a short or long value, bad padding) is refused, and the server logs `private-data-key-missing` or `private-data-key-invalid` once, never the value, and opens nothing. `GET /api/private/key` is back (public, cached 5 minutes, 404 without a key). `ADMIN_PASSWORD` is no longer used for the private data. **Security**: offline password guessing is gone, because the key is random and the public key is not a verifier of anything guessable. If the key leaks, set a new `PRIVATE_DATA_KEY` and run `node tools/private_data.mjs reseal`; old commits stay readable with the old key, so rotation protects only files sealed after it. Tampering, a swapped file name and a wrong key still fail GCM authentication. The key lives only in Railway: it is never in the repository, a log, a report or an endpoint. |

| 2026-11-07 | **Weather and time of day: Auto (the default), sunny, overcast, rain and night; alone each player picks it in the settings, together the admin picks it for everyone; "Next weather" and a faster cycle** (Yoshi: "together, the admin picks the mood for everyone from the menu; alone, each player picks it in their settings"; "I also want the weather to automatically cycle, but don't rush it too much"; "Fast forward should also be possible for the admin in production") | `src/world/weather.ts` holds each mood as a set of numbers and a sky photo. Auto: the moods follow each other by themselves, each holding 20 to 40 minutes (the night 10 to 20), sunny most often, then overcast, rain, night, never the same twice and never sunny straight to night or back (clouds come between); each change blends over two and a half minutes (light, haze, eyes, rain fading in and out, the ground drying slowly after rain). The order and lengths come from a seed (`shared/moods.mjs` MoodCycle): together the server's, so every page works out the same weather from the server's clock (the server sends the seed, the cycle's own clock and pace, its time, and where the cycle is now, on joining and on each change); alone the page's. A mood picked by hand blends in over eight seconds. "Next weather" ends the mood shown on Auto and starts the usual blend into the next: alone in the settings, together the admin's for everyone, who can also run the cycle 10 or 60 times as fast (back to normal on a fixed mood or when the last admin leaves). The dev build has `briel.weatherAhead(seconds)`. The overcast and night skies (Poly Haven, CC0, 1k, about a megabyte each) are fetched only when a mood needs them (on Auto as soon as the next mood is known); the sky seen crossfades between two photos (the second read only while they blend); the sky's light swaps over halfway with its strength kept to the blend; their light maps are made at the sunny sky's size, so nothing compiles again (no stall). Under clouds the sun's light fades out and its shadow map is no longer drawn; at night it is a weak blue moon from the sun's own side, so the baked shadows still agree. The bakes are not baked again: one shared uniform scales their sky light to the sky shown, presses their contrast together and darkens wet walls. Rain: one draw call of thin streaks round the eye (2500 on Low and phones, up to 9000 on Ultra), none indoors, the ground wet through one uniform (darker, roads and paving shiny, most in the hollows), its sound made in code. Night: the street lamps glow and light the ground under them (two draw calls, no real lights), birdsong, rooks and the pheasant are quiet, the wind softer. `src/world/mood.ts` exports the mood shown (`mood.night`, `mood.wet` blended each frame, `onMoodChange`) for the lit windows. Measured on this PC's integrated chip at the church square (shared with other agents): sunny, rain and night within about 1 ms of each other on Low and High. **Security**: three new WebSocket messages, `{ t: 'mood', mood }`, `{ t: 'moodskip' }` and `{ t: 'moodspeed', speed }`. Attack classes: a forged admin message (privilege escalation) and message flooding. The server takes them only from a connection whose session's role is admin (the role comes from the login, never from the message); `mood` only a name from the fixed list (`isChoice`: a string, exactly one of auto and the four moods), `speed` only 1, 10 or 60, both only on Auto where they apply; at most one choice or pace a second and one "Next weather" every five seconds per admin, on top of the existing flood guard; the same value again tells nobody. A skip moves only the cycle's own clock forward to the start of the next blend, so it can't jump the weather. Everything is kept in memory only, logged nowhere, and sent to everyone and in each welcome. The page checks every value again (choice, cycle, time) and shows the admin's name in the chat as text. `server/mood.test.mjs` tests the role, forged fields, values off the list, the rates, the reset of the pace, the welcome and the cycle itself. |

Behind each window is a room drawn per pixel of glass from the view ray (interior mapping): its own depth, walls, floor, a cabinet or sofa, a picture, sometimes a door, about one in ten with the curtains closed, under the existing curtains and the sky in the glass (`world/windows.ts`). It replaces the car's old hum. **Camera**: it drops back up to half a metre as the car speeds up and closes in as it brakes, swings a little out of a turn, rides the bumps and leans a quarter of the body's roll. | 2026-11-07 | **A football on the school's playground between two small goals, and one on the back lawn; the server owns the ball together** (brief: "a football to kick on the school's playground", own physics, server-authoritative) | **Physics** (`web/shared/ball.mjs`, run by the server and the page alike, no library, as "Own simple collision instead of a physics library"): a 21 cm youth ball with gravity, a little air drag, bounces off the relief (shared/terrain.mjs), rolling resistance (a kick rolls 30 to 40 m) and sleep when still; no spin. Each pitch is a small world of its own, the same on both sides: the outline the ball stays inside (the playground's walls, fences and sheds, the gate closed to the ball; the lawn's hedges and borders), upright posts, round bars and flat panels (the play tower, the balance posts and beam, the benches, the goals' frames and nets). Steps are cut so the ball moves at most a third of its radius per substep (no tunnelling through a post or a net at any kick or frame time), and its middle is never let outside the outline. **Goals:** the school's model already had a small goal at the south end (`tools/school.py`); ours at the gate end is its twin, made in code (one mesh, square tubes and cords), its net clear of the corridor's benches. A goal: a short cheer made in code at the goal, "Goal!" with the score for those within 80 m, and after 1.6 s in the net the ball is back in the middle. The score goes back to 0-0 after ten minutes untouched. **Kicking:** walking into the ball pushes it ahead; Use next to it (E, Triangle, the Use button; a click too where it doesn't shoot: Explore and the school grounds) kicks it where you look, a little above it (looking at the ball keeps it low), 9 m/s, 14 m/s on the run. A door you look at, a tag or a revive goes first; the ball goes before a car or a horse. Not driving, riding, flying or dead. Bullets and paint go through it; zombies ignore it. **Together** (`web/server/football.mjs`): the server runs both balls, walks every player on foot near one into it from their last position to their new one, and takes kicks as requests (`kickball`). States go out ten times a second while a ball moves (at once after a kick, a goal or a hard bounce, once when it comes to rest) and only to players within 250 m of it. The page runs the ball ahead of the server from each state, by its age on the server's clock (net/track.ts), so its own kicks and pushes show at once; it passes over the server's older states until its kick is acknowledged, and goes back to the server's ball when it isn't. Alone the page runs the same code. **Security:** remote kick forgery: a kick counts only within reach of the server's ball by the server's own position of the kicker (1.3 m plus 0.8 m for the delay of their updates, height checked), alive, on foot, not flying or under a parachute; positions in the message are ignored. Impulse forgery: a kick sets the velocity, never adds to it; it is capped at 9 m/s, or 14 only when the server measured the kicker running at 2.2 m/s or more over the last 0.2 s; never down, never steeper than 43 degrees; only finite numbers in a three-number array. Flooding: one kick per 200 ms per player, under the existing per-player message limits; a refused kick is not answered. Ball ids are checked against the server's own map (no prototype lookups). No new data is collected or logged. **Checks:** 15 Node tests of the physics (finite under any step, NaN or spike; rests and sleeps; stays inside after 3,000 kicks as hard and steep as allowed; never through a post, the bar or a net; goals count once; pushes; reach; fair kicks) and 9 of the server (forged far kick, kicks from above, while dead, driving or riding, impulse over the cap, flooding, malformed messages, two players get the same states, goals). Headless on this PC: kicks with E into both goals, off a post and the long block's wall, on High and Low, touch emulation with the Use button, SwiftShader (no black surfaces); two players on a local built server: both kick, both see the ball in the same place within 5 cm, a forged far kick is refused, a kick the server refused goes back on the kicker's page. **Cost** (ball and goal shown and hidden alternated, 4 runs of 120 frames after a warm-up, the chip shared with other agents): Low 2.54 ms hidden, 2.59 shown; Medium 2.56 hidden, 2.57 shown; 0.02 ms of CPU per frame. Not tried on a real phone or controller. |

Its moving parts are apart (frame, fork and bars about the steering axis, two wheels, the crank, two pedals kept level, the stand), and every bike's part is one instance of one mesh painted per vertex, the paint tinted per bike (seven colours): all thirteen bikes are ten draws. **Riding** (`world/bike.ts`): Use (E, Triangle, touch Use) next to one, again to get off on its left. W, R2 or the stick forward pedal, S or L2 brake and, standing, walk it back at 1 m/s; A and D steer (full lock at a crawl, a third of it at 15 km/h). A rider's power takes it to 25 km/h on the flat in about six seconds; the slope under the two wheels (the relief) pulls on it and changes what pedalling holds: on Molenhoek's 2 % stretch 21 km/h up and 29 km/h down, 36 km/h at most anywhere. It leans into the turn as far as speed and turn ask (at most 31 degrees), the bars turn, the wheels roll, the cranks go round while pedalled and stand still on the freewheel, the stand folds up. The camera stays close behind, over the rider's head, swings back after you look round, and leans a little (not with reduced motion). It stops against the world, trees and people like a car but is 0.62 m wide: it rides the side passage, over kerbs up to 20 cm and up onto Molenhoek's raised cycle lanes. Your own figure (`net/swat.ts`) sits on it, hands on the grips, feet on the pedals, bent over the bars. The jump key (Space, Cross, touch Bell) rings a bell made in code (`world/bikeSound.ts`), with the tyres' rush and the freewheel's ticking; nothing downloaded. Its keys in the Controls tab and the prompts follow keyboard, controller or touch; on touch the buttons are laid out as in the car, Bell where Brake is. Text in English and Flemish Dutch. **Rules, as the car's:** in Explore, Paintball and Action; the rider can't shoot and can't be shot, no doors, no flying, no parachute, no portal (get off first, as from a horse); not during Zombies. It hurts nobody. A bike stays where it is left until the server restarts. **Multiplayer:** the server keeps each bike (`server/game.mjs`): who rides it and where; the rider's state is the bike's, sent in the ticks (`shared/wire.mjs`: `k` [id, age, x, y, z, yaw, rider], and a bike flag on the rider's figure), drawn a little behind as the cars (`net/track.ts`; 400 frames watched in a second client: no jumps, at most 9 cm off an even ride). How it leans, steers and is pedalled each page works out from how it moves, so nothing more travels. **Security:** two new messages, each with its own checks. `bike` (on, off): at most three a second (shared with the cars and horses), a known id only (`Object.hasOwn`, strings: no `__proto__`), and getting on only for a living player on foot (no car, horse or other bike, not flying or in a drop) within the bike's half diagonal plus 1.5 m and 1.5 m of its height, on a bike nobody rides: taking a ridden bike (theft, or pushing its rider off) is refused, and an `off` frees only your own. `bell`: only from a bike's rider, at most every 400 ms, and the server sends out only that bike's id, never a place a page gives (no bells rung from elsewhere, no message flood to every page). | 2026-11-07 | **One sun shadow map that follows the eye on Medium and up, the bakes handing over to it everywhere, and a soft glow round bright light** (batch 4, part L: better shadows and bloom, picked by measurement) | **Shadows.** The live map stayed on the main house, so away from it nothing that moves cast a shadow. Not cascades: three's CSM replaces the materials' own shader changes (ground shade, baked light, windows) and adds a shadow pass per cascade, and a coarse far map would only repeat the bakes, which already hold the shadows of everything that stands still. The village's tiles cast into the map when it follows, only those round its middle. The map is drawn once a frame: before, every draw of the scene drew it again, the ambient occlusion's too. **Glow** (`src/bloom.ts`): what is brighter than a threshold after the exposure (0.6 at night, 2.6 by day; sunlit walls measure about 1.5) at a quarter of the resolution (a half on Ultra), blurred down to a 64th of the screen and back, added in the output pass before tone mapping, strength following the night (1.0 at night, 0.15 by day). | 2026-11-07 | **Split screen at /splitscreen: two to four players on one computer, a copy of the game per player in one page, each seat with its own session cookie** (Yoshi: "the fast route"; "first let them choose 2, 3 or 4 players, then let them connect the controllers, and then proceed; make this user friendly") | A page linked from nowhere (noindex) shows up to four iframes of the game: 2 side by side, 3 or 4 in two rows. Four steps, all doable with a controller: how many; the controllers (the first pad to press a button is player 1, its card lights up with its kind and name and it rumbles; player 1 may take the keyboard and mouse); the names (typed or suggested) and the PIN once (with a number pad); Start, which joins the seats one by one with each card's progress and any refusal on that card. A copy learns its seat from its frame (`frameElement`, which the browser shows only to a page of the same origin, so nothing is in the URL), reads only its own controller by index from the top page, keeps its settings in memory (seats 2 to 4 muted; nothing reaches the normal game's settings), picks graphics with the frame budget shared by the views (Medium at most), and loads half-size textures with 3 or 4 views, as a phone does (with four copies, about 0.3 GB less in the graphics process and 0.5 GB in the page's; each copy still holds about 0.9 GB there, so four are close to a 4 GB chip). **Security.** *Cross-seat session confusion:* the four frames share one cookie jar, so each seat gets its own session cookie, `__Host-briel_seat1` to `4` (HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict, Path=/, 12 h), set only by a join or login that names the seat. A request names its seat in the X-Briel-Seat header (the WebSocket in its subprotocol `briel-seat-N`, the only header a page can give it), never in the URL; it is then answered from that seat's cookie only, never the normal session's, and a request without a seat from the normal cookie only. A malformed seat gets no session (and a 400 from the API). The header only chooses among cookies the browser already holds: naming seat 2 without its cookie gets nobody. *Session fixation:* tokens are still made by the server only, and HttpOnly cookies can't be written by script. *Cookie tossing:* the `__Host-` prefix keeps a sibling subdomain from planting a seat cookie. *CSRF on the join:* SameSite=Strict, the Origin check on every POST and on the WebSocket, and a custom header that another site can't send without CORS, which this server never grants. *Clickjacking:* only the game's own page may now be framed, and only by this origin (`frame-ancestors 'self'`, X-Frame-Options SAMEORIGIN); every other page stays `'none'`/DENY, and nothing a visitor uploads is ever served as a page. The PIN stays in the split-screen page's memory, is sent once per seat to /api/join and is forgotten after. Every existing check holds: wrong PINs count per browser, per address and overall (a wrong PIN stops the remaining joins, so it counts once); the 16-player cap, the name rules and kicks apply per seat. Correct joins have no rate limit, so four from one address trip nothing, and no limit was raised. A kick of one seat keeps the whole PC (one browser) from joining new names for its 10 minutes; the other seats play on. Tests in `server/seats.test.mjs`. **Performance** (this PC, chip shared with other agents): the iframes share one renderer process and one main thread, about 4 ms per copy per frame on a Ryzen 9 9950X3D; 4 copies keep that thread 100 % busy at 55 fps, where 4 separate windows each hold 60 at about 27 %. On a laptop like the XPS 15 (i7-10750H) that thread caps 3 seats near 40-45 fps and 4 near 30. Separate windows would lift it, but Chrome opens one pop-up per click, only one window can be full screen, and gamepads in unfocused windows need a real test; the grid stays for now. |

| 2026-11-07 | **Player accounts, Zombies rooms and a board of best runs; no PIN** (Yoshi: "I want people to log in with username and passwords ... start like a zombie game which opens some kind of a room ... other people can join it without making the entire session switch to zombie mode ... I still want people to be able to join without having a user ... Their username should be unique. They should give an email address, which I will not use yet ... a persistent high score"; then "only people with a user can create a room. Guests can join rooms but cannot create them ... just get rid of the pin. Rooms and the open world do not see each other. Only accounts on the board. You can remove the old users table") | Replaces "No player accounts" (30 Oct) and the rows on the shared PIN. **How it plays.** Together on the start screen: you play as a guest under the name you typed, with "I'm 13 or older" ticked, or you log in or make an account from a card (`src/ui/account.ts`). An account is a unique name (the guests' rules, lower case, so "Yoshi" and "yoshi" are one), an email address that is only kept, and a password of at least 8 characters that isn't the name or a common one. It keeps your name, puts your Zombies runs on the board and lets you open a room. In the menu's Together tab ("Your account") a player sees their email, sets a new password or deletes the account with everything in it. A forgotten password: the admin gives a new one on brieltje.be/admin (Accounts tab: new password shown once, off, on, delete). **Rooms** (`server/rooms.mjs`, `src/ui/rooms.ts`): an account or the admin opens a room at one of the six Zombies places; everyone together sees the list (who opened it, where, players, the wave) and anyone, guests too, joins. A room is a game of its own on the server (a second `Game` with `room` set): its own players, doors, panes and zombies, so the open world never switches to Zombies and the two never see each other. Its players stay inside its place's edge; anyone in it starts Zombies, only accounts and the admin stop a running game. At most 4 rooms and 8 players each, one per owner; an empty room closes after 90 s. The open world's furniture, Teslas, rules and weather are copied into a room and can't be changed from it. **The board:** each account's best run per place (wave, then zombies beaten; team size and day), never a chosen wave, shown to everyone in the Together tab's Scores with a filter per place (`GET /api/scores`). The fights' totals now count accounts only; the totals kept by typed names were deleted, and so was the old `users` table. A guest's Zombies end screen says an account keeps the score. **Decided by me (Yoshi gave no answer):** one board with a place filter, rooms of 8 and at most 4, accounts unused for 2 years deleted. **Security.** Attack classes: online password guessing and credential stuffing, account enumeration, lockout DoS, sign-up and name squatting floods, CPU exhaustion through scrypt, session fixation and revocation races, CSRF, IDOR on rooms. Passwords: scrypt (N=16384, 16 MB), at most 2 checks at once and 16 waiting, the rest "busy". Login answers the same for an unknown name and a wrong password, and an unknown name costs a check against a dummy hash. Wrong passwords: 20 per address and 10 per account in 15 minutes; a browser that logged in to that account before (`__Host-briel_acct`, signed for the account's name, creation time and a hash of its password hash, so it stops counting after a new password or a re-made name) skips the account's count, so guessers can't lock the owner out. Sign-ups: 20 per address and 300 in all per hour; guest joins: 40 per address per 15 minutes and 3000 guest sessions at once. Every revoke (new password, reset, off, delete, kick) raises the account's revocation count first; a login or password change notes it before reading and gets no session if it moved, and every new session re-reads the account. Sessions are random tokens kept as SHA-256 hashes, 30 days for accounts (at most 10), HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict, `__Host-`; every call checks Origin and takes JSON only. Account names can't be taken by guests or by the admin's login (asked only after the right admin password). A room id is 12 random characters, grants nothing (the session cookie does), and only accounts and the admin open one; a room refuses every message that would change the open world or move a player out. The email is shown only to its owner and the admin, never in the game; the privacy policy, terms and legitimate interest assessment say what is kept and for how long. Automated security reviews found and fixed a device cookie outliving a password change and three races between a login and a revoke; the review before merging found and fixed rooms keeping old rules (rule changes now reach every room), rooms that only an empty room's timeout closed (the admin closes one from brieltje.be/admin), stray rows on the fights' board (joined to accounts now), an admin login racing a sign-up, and a full room's page retrying for ever. Tested: 230+ server tests (accounts, rooms, auth, seats), the build, and a local server with two headless browsers and a third client (sign-up, a room at the church, a guest joining and starting Zombies, the open world never switching, leaving, the account card, the board, a phone). |
| 2026-11-07 | **The admin's two-step login, logging in on a console with the phone, and sortable admin tables** (Yoshi: "make TOTP MFA possible to set for the admin account (user friendly!)", "Can we implement something like sign in by scanning qr code for console players so they don't have to type?", "make the tables in the admin side super user friendly, filtering, sorting, etc.") | **Two-step login** (`server/adminMfa.mjs`): brieltje.be/admin, Security: "Turn on" shows a QR code, a link that opens the authenticator app on the same phone and the key to type; one code confirms it, then 8 recovery codes are shown once (Copy, "I have kept them"). From then on the admin page and the game's Admin link ask for the 6-digit code after the right password. Lost phone and codes: change ADMIN_PASSWORD in Railway; at its next start the server turns two-step login off and logs it. **QR codes** are our own encoder (`server/qr.mjs`, byte mode, level M, versions 1 to 10), checked against the standard's worked example and decoded by a reader for every length. **Phone login** (`server/pairing.mjs`, `web/public/pair/`): "On a console? Log in with your phone" shows a QR code and a code like K7QH-3MXP; the phone opens brieltje.be/pair, logs in to its account there if needed, sees "Log in the other screen as …?" and taps yes; the console is logged in within two seconds. **Tables**: every admin table sorts by a click on its heading (again: the other way), keeps the order when redrawn, and says "12 of 40 shown"; Accounts also filters on, off or playing now. The PIN form is gone from the admin page (the row above). **Security.** Attack classes: TOTP brute force and replay, a password oracle through "a code is needed", step-up bypass (re-enrolling without a code), secret disclosure from a database leak; device-code phishing, code guessing, session theft through the short code, CSRF on approve, Host header poisoning of the QR link, URL leakage. Two-step: a 160-bit secret, kept AES-256-GCM encrypted with a key made from ADMIN_PASSWORD by scrypt with a stored random salt (the password is never in the database, and guessing it from a copy is as slow as from its hash); a code is taken in its 30 s and one step either side, compared in constant time, and a used step is refused; every wrong password counts, the right one without a code doesn't (it is no secret to whoever typed it), and wrong codes count like wrong admin passwords (10 per address in 15 minutes, 20 an hour in all, and 5 per admin session for turning it off or new recovery codes); the code is checked last, so a refused name never uses one up; recovery codes are 10 evenly drawn characters kept as an HMAC with that key, each used once; turning it on ends every other admin session; setting it up again, turning it off and new recovery codes all need a code (a review found and closed re-enrolling without one); an unreadable secret keeps the admin locked rather than quietly off, and a missing ADMIN_PASSWORD at start keeps it for when the password is back (only a changed one turns it off). Pairing: the console keeps a 256-bit secret (stored as a SHA-256 hash, sent only in a same-origin JSON POST) and only it collects the session, once; the short code (8 of 31 characters, 5 minutes) alone gives nothing; the console also shows a 2-digit number that is in no link or QR code, and the phone types it before its yes counts (number matching against a link sent to someone; one wrong number ends the pairing); only an account says yes, wrong codes are limited per address and per session, pairings per address and in all; "Log out on every device" in Your account ends every session of the account; the revocation count is noted at the yes, so a new password, a switch-off or a kick before the console collects gives it nothing; the phone page names the account, warns to say yes only to a screen in front of you, and takes the code out of the address bar at once (it travels in the fragment, never to the server's logs or a Referer). The QR link uses the host the console itself asked, which only changes that console's own code. A second review found and fixed the fast key, the per-session limit, the wipe without a password, the counted login and the uneven recovery codes. Tested: unit tests (RFC 6238 values, replay, recovery, reset, limits, pairing with the number), and a headless run against a local server (setup, login with a code, a reused code refused, a table sorted, a console paired by a phone). |

| 2026-11-07 | **Five fixes from a full security review: home codes, doors, grenades, forms, broken URLs** (Yoshi chose these five from the review; the game's admin login reaching the login log and the homes with the password only, and Cloudflare Workers passing the Cloudflare-only check, stay as they are) | **Home codes** (`server/homes.mjs`). Every try ran a scrypt hash (16 MB, tens of ms) on Node's four worker threads, with no limit shared between senders, and every wrong try at a real home wrote the store. Now the checks take turns (`server/auth.mjs` `Turns`, the class the account passwords use too, with turns of their own): two at once, eight waiting, the rest hear "busy" (503, the page says the code could not be checked). Each home has a budget of 300 checks an hour from everyone together, and made-up ids share one of their own; a "busy" refusal is not counted. Past a budget nobody is refused for it: tries go one at a time through that budget's slow lane (four waiting, a second's pause before each), so a flood costs about one hash a second. A session that holds the home's grant skips the slow lane. After the hash the record is read again: a try opens the home only if it is still live with the very hash that was checked, so a code replaced, revoked or switched off while a try waited opens nothing. Wrong tries are counted in memory and written at most once a minute; the admin's list adds those not written yet (up to a minute of them is lost on a restart). The per-sender limits (30 an hour, 8 per home in ten minutes) stay. **Doors** (`server/game.mjs`). The door table took any id matching the pattern and stopped at 100, so a player could jam every door until a restart. Now only known ids count: the house data's doors (shell and the insides of the main house and the bungalow), the end house's shell and its inside when the private data holds it. The village has no doors. The table can't grow past that list, so the cap of 100 went. **Grenades** (`server/grenade.mjs`). A blast point anywhere within 60 m of the throw was taken. Now it is pulled back to speed x (fuse + 0.5 s), about 49 m, from where the server had the thrower, and to the throw's carried ground speed x that time when the message has one; it can't sit higher than a throw climbs. Falling from a roof is not limited. **Forms** (game night, interest, feedback). The per-sender limits count an IPv6 sender per /56 (`formBucketOf`), since a home connection often holds 256 /64s; logins keep `bucketOf` (/64). The admin's deletes of a sign-up and of a comment now go through the same Origin check as the other admin calls. **Broken %-escapes** in a URL path (`/%`) get a quiet 400 instead of a 500 and a log line (`server/paths.mjs`). **Security:** the attack classes are resource exhaustion (CPU and worker threads by scrypt floods, store writes, log flooding) and its form of denial of service (jammed doors, filled form caps), hit forgery (a forged blast point), a time-of-check to time-of-use race (a replaced code still opening a home) and CSRF (a cross-site delete by the logged-in admin's browser). Why it is safe: no check got weaker. The unlock flood is bounded at every step before any hash. A flood at one home slows only that home's tries, never refuses its owner for an hour, and 50 bits of code stay hopeless to guess at about one check a second. The admin needs no code at all. Door ids come only from the server's own data, never from a page. The blast clamp uses the server's position, so a page can only make a blast nearer, never further. The /56 is stricter than the /64 for one household and changes nothing for IPv4. The Origin check is the one `body()` already does for every other admin change. Nothing new is collected or logged except `home_unlock_capped` (at most once an hour for each budget, with the home's id or '-', no address). |
| 2026-11-07 | **Faster loading, part 2: play 12 s to under 5 s after the click at the church** (Yoshi chose this from the performance check) | **Shaders:** the game no longer draws behind the start screen (its slideshow covers the canvas); drawing there compiled 174 shader programs one by one on the page's thread, 5.8 s, and held up everything else. The scene compiles with `compileAsync` (in parallel where the browser has `KHR_parallel_shader_compile`) once the sky is in and again once the textures and the houses' baked light are, with a stand-in for your figure under the parachute; the graphics test or one frame behind the start screen draws the rest. It is now one permuted list of faces and typed arrays per mesh: 37 MB in place of 151 MB for 2 M triangles. **Downloads:** the town takes the game's own brick and plaster textures (`houseMeshes.ts` `sharedTexture`: a copy at another tile size shares the picture, one texture on the chip), `horse.glb` is fetched once (`world/horseModel.ts`), and Molenhoek's and Kapellenhoek's baked light comes after play begins unless you start at Molenhoek (a trip into the village waits for it, six seconds at most). Their models still come before play: the village's blocks are fitted to them before its tiles are built. Measured cold, cache off, at the church: dev build 12.9 s to 4.8 s; production build 12.2 s to 4.5 s, 36.2 MB to 29.9 MB before play; from the house 7.1 s to 2.8 s. The graphics chip was shared with other agents. |
| 2026-11-07 | **Frame rate on weak graphics chips: simple trees on Low and phones, the ambient occlusion from the scene's own depth, one snow program** (Yoshi chose the frame-rate work after a performance check on this PC's integrated Radeon) | Measured on the Radeon (`--force_low_power_gpu`), alternating before and after in one session, with other agents sharing the chip. **Trees** (`world/surroundings.ts` `setTreeDetail`, from `applyQuality`): Low runs at 12 to 15 ms however small the screen, so triangles and draw calls set its frame time, not pixels. On Low and phones the near trees (round the house and the church) take the simple 180-triangle crown the far ones have, instead of 980, and every trunk drops its forks (20 triangles instead of 60). The shapes swap in place, so the tiles stay as they are. About 140k fewer of Low's 1.3M triangles at the church, the house and Molenhoek; the church on Low 14.2-14.6 ms to 12.7-13.1, a phone-sized view at the house 10.1-10.5 ms to 9.3-10.1. Leaving out every other leafy lump on the house's hedges saved only 20k more and dotted their sides, so the hedges stay. Medium and up unchanged. **Ambient occlusion** (`main.ts`, High and Ultra only, as before): the pass drew the whole scene a second time for its own depth and normals. The composer's targets now keep the scene's depth in a texture each; the pass reads it and works the normals out from it (GTAO from depth, three.js `setGBuffer`). High at the church 976-985 draw calls and 2.9M triangles to 510-517 and 1.5M, 32-35 ms to 28-31; the house 29-31 ms to 27-27.6. Its list of things to leave out of that second drawing (fences, glass, sprites, zombies) is gone: the scene's depth has them as drawn. The name tags, role labels and health bars (sprites drawn on top of everything) no longer write depth, so the occlusion does not take them for cards in the air. **Snow** (`world/snowCover.ts` `snowy`): how white a surface gets and whether it is foliage were written into the shader and keyed the program; they are uniforms now, one program for all. Programs on High at the church 171 to 161 (with the AO's own gone). Left alone: about 40 of the 170-odd programs are compiled twice at the start, once before the sky's environment light is in and once after (the loading work's part). The normal maps are already UASTC with RDO (lambda 2); lambda 4 saved 7-11 % per file for 3.7 dB less, lambda 8 25 % for 7.5 dB less, and the one 2k map is the main house's brick seen up close, so they stay. |

| 2026-11-08 | **Driving: tyres that grip, a real handbrake, ABS** (Yoshi: "driving mechanics, improve them, I want a 'hard brake' as well like handrem") | The car was a bicycle that always turned where its wheels pointed. Now each axle's tyres push it (world/carGrip.ts): sideways up to what the ground grips, shared with braking and pulling away, the weight moving to the front as it brakes. So it runs a little wide when a corner is taken too fast (about 0.85 to 1 g on dry asphalt), more on a wet road (mood.wet, read only), gravel and grass, and the back steps out a little when you lift off or brake in a turn; a light stability control brings it back. The brake has ABS: about 0.9 g dry, 0.67 wet, and it still steers. The handbrake (Space, the pad's south button, BRAKE on touch) locks the rear wheels and brakes the front as hard as ABS lets: about 0.88 g in a straight line (11 m from 50 km/h dry, 16 m wet); with the wheel turned the front brakes less and the back swings round, about 80 degrees from 40 km/h, and grips again once let go. No stability control while it is held. The driving hint now names its key. Off the pedals the motor slows the car a little (1 m/s2, as a Tesla does); the pedals ease in over a tenth of a second; the wheel comes back to the middle a little faster than it turns in. The camera follows where the car goes while it slides, and looks a touch into the turn. The squeal grows with how fast the rubber slides. Nothing new goes over the network: a driven car still sends its place and heading, and its speed, sliding or not, never passes its top speed, so it stays inside the server's movement budget (CAR_SPEED) unchanged. |
